- [x] **M73** (medium/bug) `internal/mcp/registry.go:35` - The copy here (and the identical one in `List`) is only a shallow struct copy: `copied.InputSchema` still points at the same `map[string]any` held in `r.tools`. The copy exists to isolate callers from the registry's stored definition, but the isolation is incomplete — any caller that mutates the returned schema (e.g. injecting `properties`/`required` before serving `tools/list`) silently corrupts the shared tool definition for every other consumer. Either deep-copy the schema or document the map as immutable.
- [x] **M74** (medium/bug) `internal/mcp/server.go:65` - When `ServeStreams` returns before the input reaches EOF (e.g. `enc.Encode` fails on the next request, or the parent context is cancelled), the goroutine below is likely blocked inside `scanner.Scan()` on a blocking read. `ctx.Done()` cannot interrupt that read, so the goroutine plus the scanner (buffer grows up to 32 MB) stays alive until the reader produces data or is closed. This is harmless for `Serve()`/os.Stdin where the process exits, but it leaks per call for the exported `ServeStreams` (tests, embedded use with a pipe/socket that stays open). Consider closing the input when it implements `io.Closer` (or documenting clearly that the caller owns closing `in` to unblock the reader).
- [x] **M75** (medium/bug) `internal/metadata/metadata.go:212` - `Close()` shuts the pool down but leaves `p.pool` non-nil, so `Enabled()` (which is defined as `p.pool != nil`) keeps returning true after shutdown. Any `Record*` call that happens after `Close()` then runs `Exec` against a closed pool and returns a "closed pool" error instead of the silent no-op the `Recorder` contract implies (compare `Disabled`, whose `Enabled()` is false and whose `Record*` are no-ops). This breaks the `if d.Meta.Enabled() { record }` gating used by callers (e.g. internal/tools/evaluation.go) for any shutdown path that still has in-flight work. Clear the field (or track a closed flag) so `Enabled()` reflects reality.
- [x] **M76** (medium/security) `internal/objectstore/minio.go:46` - Empty credentials are silently accepted here. `SettingsFromEnv` allows `MINIO_ACCESS_KEY`/`MINIO_SECRET_KEY` to be unset while `MINIO_ENDPOINT` is configured, so `Open` proceeds with `NewStaticV4("", "", "")`. Depending on the minio-go version this degrades to unauthenticated/anonymous access (cross-tenant data exposure) or to an opaque `SignatureDoesNotMatch` that hides the real misconfiguration. Fail fast on a partially configured credential pair.
- [x] **M77** (medium/bug) `internal/objectstore/minio.go:56` - The `BucketExists` -> `MakeBucket` sequence is a check-then-act that races when two service replicas start concurrently (e.g. scaled docker-compose / multiple pods against the same MinIO). The loser gets `BucketAlreadyOwnedByYou`/`BucketAlreadyExists`, and because the error is returned verbatim the caller disables the object store entirely (see `cmd/hackathonkit-mcp/main.go:162`), silently losing artifact mirroring. Treat an already-existing bucket as success.
- [x] **M78** (medium/performance) `internal/objectstore/minio.go:76` - `io.ReadAll` loads the whole remote object into memory with no size bound. `Get` is reachable from `Store.ReadArtifact` (internal/storage/store.go:192) whenever the local file is missing, and the object name/size in the bucket is not under this process's control — a large or hostile object (shared/default bucket, leftover upload) will allocate unbounded memory and can OOM the daemon. The `Stat` call above already returns the size; use it to reject oversized objects (or cap via `io.LimitReader`).
- [x] **M79** (medium/bug) `internal/observability/obs.go:84` - A non-marshalable field (channel, func, map with non-string keys, NaN, ...) makes `Log` return silently, and the `l.Println` write error is discarded as well. For an observability helper this means the record disappears with no signal at all, which is exactly the failure mode you cannot debug later. Emit a guaranteed-marshalable fallback entry instead of dropping the event (or return an error the caller can surface).
- [x] **M80** (medium/bug) `internal/observability/obs.go:153` - The read window does not always reach the start of the file (it stops early on the newline-count break or when `len(buf) >= maxTailBytes`), so the first element returned by `splitLines(buf)` is the *tail of a record that began before the window*. `json.Valid` only proves the fragment parses as standalone JSON, not that it is a whole record, so a scalar/nested fragment can be emitted as a bogus entry (this feeds the demo-api tail endpoint). Drop the leading element whenever the window started mid-file (`offset > 0` after the loop).
- [x] **M81** (medium/performance) `internal/openai/client.go:208` - `Transcribe` buffers the entire audio file into a `bytes.Buffer` (a second full copy on top of the file) with no size limit. Unlike `vision.analyze`, the `audio.transcribe` tool path (internal/tools/extras.go:473) does not cap the input size, so a large media file drives an unbounded allocation per call. Add a size check on the file before copying (e.g. reject files above a documented maximum).
- [x] **M82** (medium/bug) `internal/openai/client.go:227` - The read error is discarded. If the response body is truncated mid-transfer (network reset, connection drop), `data` holds a partial body and the failure is either masked by a confusing `json.Unmarshal` error or, worse, silently reported as an empty/short transcript. Propagate the read error like `doJSON` does.
- [x] **M83** (medium/bug) `internal/plans/plans.go:85` - `len(out)` counts bytes, but the allowlist above admits multi-byte runes (Cyrillic `а`–`я`, `ё`), so `out[:60]` can cut a rune in half and produce a file stem with invalid UTF-8. Example: 59 ASCII chars followed by `аб` yields 63 bytes and the slice ends between the two bytes of `а`. The invalid stem is then used as a filename by `Path`/`Save` (plan.create accepts arbitrary caller slugs), and `json.Marshal` rewrites the invalid byte in the stored `slug` field as U+FFFD, so the JSON slug no longer matches the file stem. Truncate by rune count instead.
- [x] **M84** (medium/performance) `internal/quality/quality.go:83` - `CombinedOutput` accumulates the full stdout+stderr of every check in memory before `tail` trims it to 2000 bytes. `go test ./...` on a verbose or looping package (or a runaway script) can produce hundreds of MB, so the gate can OOM the daemon/container even though only the tail is ever persisted. Stream the output into a bounded ring buffer (or a temp file) and keep only the last N bytes.
- [x] **M85** (medium/security) `internal/quality/quality.go:135` - `sha` is joined straight into the output path with no validation, and `filepath.Join` cleans `..`, so a value like `../../x` escapes `evals/quality` (and `Save` then writes outside the intended directory). The value is not necessarily trusted: `GitSHA` returns whatever it reads from `.git/HEAD`/`packed-refs` without checking that it is a hex object name, so a crafted repo (or a caller that passes a `Report` decoded from JSON) can drive the path. Validate the SHA shape (e.g. `^[0-9a-f]{7,40}$`) and fall back to `unknown` otherwise, or reject it in `Save`.
- [x] **M86** (medium/bug) `internal/quality/quality.go:166` - `Latest` decides freshness from the directory `ModTime` (which also changes when unrelated files are written/removed in `evals/quality/<sha>/`) and silently skips unreadable or corrupt `gate.json` files. A newer, unparsable report therefore makes the function fall back to an older one without any signal, and the MCP `quality.gate` tool then reports stale SHA/OK evidence as current. Prefer ordering by the report's own `FinishedAt` and surface (or at least distinguish) entries that failed to read/decode instead of dropping them silently.
- [x] **M87** (medium/maintainability) `internal/resources/matrix.go:61` - The authoritative config is decoded with `yaml.Unmarshal`, which ignores unknown/typo'd fields. A misspelled capability key or a `provider` field typo is accepted silently and quietly changes planning behavior (unknown capabilities simply fall through to the skip path; unknown providers are treated as never ready). Decode with a strict decoder (`yaml.NewDecoder(...).KnownFields(true)`, requires importing `bytes`) so configuration mistakes fail loudly.
- [x] **M88** (medium/maintainability) `internal/resources/matrix.go:80` - `Validate` checks track ids and capability names but never validates `Variant.Provider` against the known provider ids (`Defs()`). `Ready` returns false for an unrecognized provider id, so a typo'd provider silently forces permanent degradation while `providerMissing` returns nil for it (no diagnostic). Validate provider ids here so a bad provider reference is reported at load time.
- [x] **M89** (medium/bug) `internal/resources/matrix.go:103` - Capabilities with no configured variants are silently dropped from `decisions` while still present in `resolved.Capabilities`, so `resolved` and `decisions` no longer align and callers that iterate decisions (e.g. `DecisionSummary`, `DegradedCapabilities`) lose coverage without any signal. `Matrix.Validate` only rejects such a matrix when it arrives through `LoadMatrix`; a matrix built in-process (e.g. `DefaultMatrix` variants or hand-constructed) reaches `Plan` unchecked. Either emit an explicit placeholder decision or return an error instead of `continue`.
- [x] **M90** (medium/bug) `internal/resources/probe.go:56` - The `io.ReadAll` error is discarded and the reader is capped at 1 MiB, so a failed read or a response body larger than 1 MiB is silently truncated. In that case `json.Unmarshal` fails, `Models` stays 0, and the function still returns `Reachable: true` with no error — a broken/unparsed response is reported as a successful probe with zero models. Capture the read failure (or flag truncation) instead of ignoring it.
- [x] **M91** (medium/bug) `internal/resources/resources.go:83` - `Defs()` only copies the `[]Def` slice header; each copied `Def` still aliases the package-level `EnvKeys` backing array. A caller that writes `Defs()[i].EnvKeys[j]` (or passes it to a helper that normalizes/redacts keys) silently corrupts `defs` for every subsequent `Statuses`/`Defs` call in the process. `Statuses` already deep-copies with `append([]string{}, d.EnvKeys...)`, so this is inconsistent. Deep-copy each `EnvKeys` here.
- [x] **M92** (medium/bug) `internal/resources/resources.go:111` - The numeric shortcut compares against the zero-padded ID prefix, so un-padded user input never resolves a track by number: `LookupTrack("1")` does not match `01-energy`, and the input then falls through to the substring fallback. Normalize both sides by trimming leading zeros before comparing.
- [x] **M93** (medium/bug) `internal/resources/resources.go:113` - The `len(ref) >= 4 && strings.Contains(label, ref)` fallback turns an arbitrary substring into a valid track and returns the *first* match in iteration order. E.g. `LookupTrack("assistant")` silently resolves to `02-finance` even though `03-management` and `05-logistics` also contain it, and `LookupTrack("ai assistant")` does the same. Callers treat `ok == true` as an unambiguous resolution (see `applyResourcesSet`/`system.resources_set`), so this can persist the wrong track. Restrict the fuzzy branch to a prefix/slug match on the theme part of the label, or collect matches and fail when more than one matches.
- [x] **M94** (medium/maintainability) `internal/resources/resources.go:158` - `Save` mixes value and reference semantics: `Version`, `UpdatedAt`, `Track` and `CodexPro` are normalized only on the local copy (the caller's profile silently keeps the stale values), while `profile.Claimed` is a map that *is* mutated in place (e.g. `profile.Claimed["codex_pro"]` is inserted below). Either take `*Profile`/return the normalized `Profile` so callers can observe canonical values, or copy `Claimed` before mutating so the parameter is truly read-only. Also note the denormalization risk: because `Claimed["codex_pro"]` wins over the `CodexPro` field here, a caller that only sets `CodexPro = true` on a profile loaded with `Claimed["codex_pro"] == false` will have it clobbered back to `false`. The duplicate `LookupTrack` call can also be collapsed into one.
- [x] **M95** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/embed.go:134` - Degenerate min-max normalization silently zeroes every category. When all cosine similarities are (nearly) equal — a zero/empty task embedding, a weak/hash embedder, or a single surviving centroid after the dimension guard — `maxSim-minSim < 1e-9`, so `rank` stays 0 for every category and `min(rank, absolute)` caps all scores at 0, discarding even a high absolute similarity (e.g. 0.9). The function still returns `(out, true)`, so the caller believes embedding scoring succeeded while the embedding signal is fully suppressed. Since the stated intent is "cap the ranking by absolute similarity", the no-spread case should fall back to the absolute similarity instead of an undefined rank.
- [x] **M96** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/events.go:17` - `kc_total` is reported inconsistently: the empty/fallback snapshot hardcodes the global `MBKenyonCells` constant, while the success path reports `brain.KC`. When a brain with a different KC is loaded (e.g. `NewMBrain(32, ...)` / a saved brain), any failure path (missing embedder, embed failure, scoring failure) reports 4133 even though the configured model has 32 cells, so consumers logging/aggregating `kc_total` cannot tell which model produced the snapshot. Consider deriving the total from the loaded brain when one is available (falling back to `MBKenyonCells` only when `brain == nil`).
- [x] **M97** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/llm.go:48` - The only validity gate is `len(clean) == 0`, so a degenerate classifier response containing all-zero (or all-equal) scores is accepted as usable. Because `Recommend` (router.go:170-181) treats `ok == true` as a real LLM signal, it appends "llm" to the encoder and multiplies every existing score by 0.4 while adding `0.6 * 0` — damping the keyword/embedding/knn/mb signal without contributing any new information. `openai.Client.ClassifyTask` returns an entry for every parsed category (including 0.0), so an LLM that answers "nothing is relevant" or emits zeros for all categories is a reachable case and can silently push routing below the threshold. Consider requiring at least one positive score before reporting success.
- [x] **M98** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/mb.go:263` - The persistence failure of a dopamine update is discarded, so `AddFeedback` returns success even though the learned weights were only kept in memory and will be lost on restart. Combined with the non-atomic `os.WriteFile` in `saveBrain` (a crash or full disk mid-write leaves a truncated JSON file that `loadBrain` then silently rejects), a failed update is indistinguishable from a successful one. Return/propagate the error (or at minimum log it) and write the file atomically (temp file + rename).
- [x] **M99** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/mb.go:292` - `Train` assumes `Readout`/`Bias` are non-nil, but nothing guarantees that for an exported method. A zero-value brain with categories set (`&MBrain{KC: 8, Categories: []string{"a"}}`) panics on the map assignment, and a brain loaded from JSON that has `kc`/`readout` but no `bias` field passes the `loadBrain` validation (`KC == 0 || len(Readout) == 0`) and then panics on `b.Bias[category] += ...`. Initialize both maps defensively at the top of `Train` (and/or in `loadBrain`) instead of relying on `NewMBrain`.
- [x] **M100** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/memory.go:137` - `os.WriteFile` truncates the target in place, so a crash or a partially flushed write leaves a truncated/invalid JSON file. Combined with `loadMemory` treating parse failures as empty (see above), the next run silently starts from an empty memory. The package already has an atomic write helper (`writeFileAtomic` in embed.go, backed by `atomicfile.Write`); use it here so readers never observe a partial file.
- [x] **M101** (medium/performance) `internal/routing/fly/memory.go:177` - `memoryMu` is still held here (`defer r.memoryMu.Unlock()`), so `applyDopamine` — which performs an embedder call (`embedTask`, potentially a network request) plus `loadBrain`/`saveBrain` disk I/O — runs entirely under the process-global memory lock. Any other Router's `AddFeedback`/`MemorySize` (which also does file I/O under the same lock) will block for the duration. Release the lock before the callback; note the deferred `Unlock` above must be removed when doing so to avoid a double-unlock panic.
- [x] **M102** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/memory.go:200` - Examples are compared without validating that their vectors were produced by the same embedding model: `memoryFile.Model` is persisted but never checked against `r.modelTag`. When the embedder/model changes, stored vectors have a different length and `cosine` silently truncates to the shorter vector, yielding meaningless similarity votes (and, through the kNN blend in router.go, wrong routing). Note `embeddingCategoryScores` in embed.go explicitly skips stale, dimension-mismatched cache entries for exactly this reason — apply the same guard here, e.g. skip examples whose `len(example.Vector) != len(taskVector)` (and/or whose stored model tag differs from `r.modelTag`).
- [x] **M103** (medium/bug) `internal/routing/fly/router.go:128` - `encoderParts` is seeded with `"keyword"` unconditionally, so `Result.Encoder` reports the keyword signal even for tasks with zero keyword hits (e.g. `"qqq zzz"` → Encoder `"keyword"`). This misleads operators reading `fly-events.jsonl` / metrics about which encoders actually contributed. Start from an empty slice and append `"keyword"` only when `keywordSignal` is true.
- [x] **M104** (medium/performance) `internal/routing/fly/router.go:140` - The task is embedded up to three times per `Recommend` for the same string: `embeddingCategoryScores` → `embedTask`, line 140 `r.embedTask`, and `mbCategoryScores` → `embedTask`. In production the embedder is a `ChainEmbedder` whose primary is the OpenAI client with no caching (`hash_encoder.go` `ChainEmbedder.Embed` always calls `Primary` first), so each call is a separate network round trip for an identical vector. Compute the task vector once at the top of `Recommend` and pass it into the downstream helpers.
- [x] **M105** (medium/security) `internal/skills/validate.go:173` - `ref` is taken verbatim from the SKILL.md text and joined into a filesystem path without sanitizing `..` or absolute components. A crafted reference such as `` `references/../../../../etc/passwd` `` (or a markdown link `](references/../../secret)`) makes the validator Stat paths outside the skill directory, and the resulting problem message echoes the escaped path. The impact is limited today (existence probing only), but the path handling should be confined to the skill directory so it cannot be turned into a disclosure/probe primitive if a later version reads the file instead of Stat-ing it.
- [x] **M106** (medium/security) `internal/storage/store.go:65` - `ensureWithin` fails open: when `filepath.EvalSymlinks(s.root)` returns an error (root not yet created, a broken symlink component, a permission error, or a symlink loop) the function returns `nil`, silently disabling the containment guard for every caller. Failing closed is safer — return the resolution error so the caller aborts instead of proceeding unchecked.
- [x] **M107** (medium/security) `internal/telegram/telegram.go:437` - The bot token is embedded in the request URL path and `c.http` uses the default redirect policy, so any 3xx response (or a non-`api.telegram.org` value of `TELEGRAM_API_BASE`) causes the token-bearing URL to be re-sent to the `Location` host, leaking the credential. Consider setting `CheckRedirect` to refuse redirects (e.g. return `http.ErrUseLastResponse`) in `New`, and validating that `settings.BaseURL` is an https URL with a trusted host.
- [x] **M108** (medium/bug) `internal/telegram/telegram.go:449` - Using `%v` on `urlErr.Err` drops the error identity, so callers cannot distinguish a context deadline/cancellation (`context.DeadlineExceeded`) or a timeout from a plain network failure, even though `urlErr.Err` never contains the token-bearing URL. Use `%w` — it preserves `errors.Is`/`errors.As` without leaking credentials.
- [x] **M109** (medium/bug) `internal/telegram/telegram.go:531` - `budget` only reserves room for the reopened `prefix`, not for the auto-generated `closers`. Because the shrink loop at line 552 is bounded by `cut > pos+1`, it cannot shrink below one body rune, so when `prefix` is large (deeply nested tags) `prefix+body+closers` can still exceed `limit` and an over-limit chunk is emitted. Telegram then rejects it with HTTP 400. Either account for the closers in the budget or fall back to returning the remaining text unmodified when no valid split can fit.
- [x] **M110** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/context.go:104` - A marshal failure silently drops the tool from `items`/`total_tokens`, while `tool_count` is still computed from `len(reg.Names())` (registry.go:50). The response then reports a tool count larger than the number of tool items and understates the budget with no indication. Collect the skipped names and add a warning (or use `tool_count: len(items of kind tool)`) so the discrepancy is visible.
- [x] **M111** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/context.go:125` - Every failure mode of the scan is silently converted into "no item": the WalkDir error, per-entry `Info`, `ReadFile` and `Rel` errors all `return nil`/`continue`, and the returned `error` of `WalkDir` is explicitly discarded with `_ =`. A missing or unreadable skills tree therefore yields an empty skill set, `by_kind.skill == 0` and a success response with no warning, which is indistinguishable from "nothing to load" for a budgeting tool whose callers rely on it as the only source of token numbers. Track the failure and surface it through the envelope warning at minimum (or return an error when the base directory cannot be read).
- [x] **M112** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/context.go:247` - The frontmatter parse is not scoped to the frontmatter block and only understands single-line scalars: a YAML block/folded description (`description: >` / `|`) or a multi-line quoted value is truncated to the operator line, so `descriptionWarn` (500 chars) is never reached for exactly the oversized descriptions it targets. Conversely, an ordinary body line starting with `description:` (e.g. inside a fenced example) is picked up as if it were frontmatter. Parse only the leading `---` ... `---` block and handle multi-line values.
- [x] **M113** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/data.go:76` - The reverse substring fallback can silently bind a requested name to an unrelated column: if the exact/fold match fails, the first header *containing* the name wins (e.g. requesting `id` resolves to `user_id` when `user_id` is the only containing header). Combined with the silent-skip behaviour in `profileColumns`, callers can get statistics/filters for the wrong column without any signal. Consider requiring an exact (case-insensitive) match, or returning the match plus a warning so callers can surface the ambiguity.
- [x] **M114** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/data.go:110` - Requested columns that cannot be resolved are silently dropped. `profileColumns` skips any name for which `columnIndex` returns -1 (unknown name, or an ambiguous partial match), so `data.inspect`/`data.analyze` return a profile that simply omits the user's column with no error or warning — the caller cannot tell an unknown column from a column that was never requested. `data.query` correctly fails with `unknown column: ...`; make the profiling path consistent, e.g. return the unresolved names and surface them as an error or warning.
- [x] **M115** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/data.go:255` - An out-of-range `limit` is silently replaced by the default 20. A caller asking for `limit: 500` gets only 20 rows even though the documented maximum is 200, which looks like a successful query with truncated data. Clamp to the documented maximum instead of dropping back to the default.
- [x] **M116** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/deps.go:94` - `strconv.ParseFloat` accepts the special strings `"NaN"`, `"Inf"`, `"+Inf"`, `"Infinity"` (case-insensitive) and returns them without error, so a non-finite value is passed straight through to callers instead of falling back to `def`. Concretely, `eval.calibrate` does `minDelta := argFloat(args, "min_delta", -1); if minDelta < 0 { minDelta = settings.MinDelta }` — with `"min_delta": "NaN"` the `NaN < 0` check is false, so a NaN threshold reaches `evaluation.Calibrate` and every comparison silently evaluates to false. Likewise `plan.go` does `int(argFloat(m, "timebox_minutes", 0))`, where `int(NaN)` is implementation-defined (a huge value on amd64) and gets persisted into the plan. Reject non-finite parsed values and fall back to `def`.
- [x] **M117** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/documents.go:158` - `docxText` decodes `word/document.xml` with no size limit: the raw zip entry is streamed into an unbounded `strings.Builder`. A crafted/degenerate DOCX (zip bomb) under `data/input` can therefore expand to arbitrary size and OOM the daemon, while PDF extraction is capped by `maxExtractedChars` and XLSX streams are capped in `internal/tools/xlsx.go` via `io.LimitReader(rc, maxXLSX*Bytes)`. Wrap the reader with a limit (and ideally stop appending once a char budget is hit) to make DOCX consistent with the other extractors.
- [x] **M118** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/evaluation.go:367` - Commission votes are only accepted when the value is a `float64`. Args decoded from JSON are float64, but programmatic callers (Go tests/internal callers) that pass integer or `json.Number` values have those votes silently dropped, which then surfaces as a misleading `missing vote count`/`votes must equal members` error from `ApplyCommission`. Fractional values are also truncated by `int(count)` without validation, so a bad vote can change the tie-break outcome instead of being rejected.
- [x] **M119** (medium/security) `internal/tools/evaluation.go:524` - `resolveRepoPath` only rejects absolute prefixes and `..` after `filepath.Clean`; it never resolves symlinks. `readReports`/`readAggregates` accept arbitrary repo-relative paths, so a symlink inside the repository pointing outside the root (e.g. `data/x -> /etc`) is still followed, letting the caller read files outside the repository; `writeRepoJSON`/`freeze_bundle` have the same gap for writes. The storage layer already guards this with `Store.ensureWithin` (EvalSymlinks + containment check), so this helper should do the same. Note EvalSymlinks fails for not-yet-existing write targets, so walk up to the nearest existing parent as `ensureWithin` does.
- [x] **M120** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/extras.go:97` - The label is selected by raw hit count (`float64(hits) > bestScore`), but `scores[category]` is normalized by the keyword-list length (`hits / len(keywords)`). These two can disagree: a category with 3 hits out of 20 keywords (score 0.15) beats a category with 1 hit out of 2 keywords (score 0.5), returning a label whose reported score is lower than another matched category. The result is self-contradictory for the caller. Select `best` using the same normalized `score` that is returned in `scores`.
- [x] **M121** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/extras.go:173` - Invalid series entries are silently discarded here (non-object items, entries whose `points` parse to zero points). Combined with the silent coercion in `parseChartPoints` (missing/wrong-typed `x`/`y` become `""`/`0`), a caller can receive a successfully rendered artifact that misrepresents the data with no warning. Consider collecting skipped entries and surfacing them via `WithWarnings`, or failing when a supplied series cannot be parsed.
- [x] **M122** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/extras.go:282` - For `chart_type == "bar"` only `series[0]` is drawn, yet the legend below iterates over every series and the response still reports `"series": len(series)`. A caller supplying multiple series (or a second series with data and an empty first series) gets a chart/response that silently misrepresents the input. Additionally, the bar x-axis labels are computed from `longest` (the series with the most points) while the bars themselves use `series[0].Points`, so labels can be misaligned with bars when `series[0]` is not the longest. Either drop/ignore extra series explicitly (and report only what is drawn), or render all series as grouped bars with labels derived from the same series used for the bars.
- [x] **M123** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/extras.go:303` - Line series are positioned by point index (`xPos(i, len(s.Points))`), so the parsed `x`/`t` values are ignored for geometry and only 3 labels of the longest series are emitted. For non-uniform or irregular x values (e.g. timestamps), the rendered chart is misleading. If an index axis is intended, validate/normalize x accordingly; otherwise map points onto x coordinates using the supplied x values.
- [x] **M124** (medium/performance) `internal/tools/files.go:88` - `os.ReadFile(full)` loads the entire text file into memory (and `string(b)` then copies it again) purely to count lines/characters. A large `.log`/`.json`/`.txt` input exhausts memory on the request path. Other extractors in this package already cap reads (see `maxExtractedChars` / `io.LimitReader` in documents.go); use the same bounded-read approach here and warn when the cap is hit.
- [x] **M125** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/plan.go:62` - `int(argFloat(...))` is an unguarded float→int conversion. `argFloat` also parses strings, and `strconv.ParseFloat` accepts `"NaN"`, `"Inf"`, `"1e30"`, so a caller can drive the conversion of NaN/Inf/out-of-range floats, which is implementation-defined in Go (e.g. NaN on amd64 yields -9223372036854775808). Negative values (or `-1`) are also stored verbatim. The package already provides `argInt`, which floors NaN to the default and clamps to the 32-bit range — use it and reject/skip out-of-range timeboxes.
- [x] **M126** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/plan.go:72` - `plan.create` does not check whether a plan with this slug already exists: `plans.Save` overwrites the file and this literal `Revision: 1` resets the revision counter, silently discarding the previous stages, statuses and evidence. That also interacts badly with the review flow: the demo-api stores plan approvals keyed by slug+revision and serves them by slug only, so an approval recorded for the clobbered (old) revision-1 plan is surfaced for the brand-new plan. Consider failing when the file exists (or carrying over/incrementing the revision) so a re-create cannot destroy an in-progress plan.
- [x] **M127** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/quality.go:17` - `quality.Latest` returns `ok == false` in three different situations: the `evals/quality` directory is absent/unreadable, no subdirectory contains a `gate.json`, or every `gate.json` it finds fails to read or fails `json.Unmarshal` (see internal/quality/quality.go:156-183, which silently `continue`s past unreadable/unparseable entries). This branch collapses all of them into `available: false` + "no gate evidence found", so a truncated/corrupt gate.json (e.g. a save that was interrupted, or a file mangled by a botched sync) is reported to the agent as if no evidence had ever been produced — hiding a real evidence-integrity failure. Consider having `Latest` distinguish "absent" from "present but unreadable/invalid" (e.g. return an error or a third state) and surface something like "gate evidence exists but could not be parsed" here instead of the not-found message.
- [x] **M128** (medium/maintainability) `internal/tools/resources.go:237` - Enrichment failures are swallowed: a malformed `configs/resource-matrix.yaml` (LoadMatrix error) or an unusable playbook (Plan error) makes the envelope silently omit `track_plan`/`variants` and report success, so the caller cannot tell that variant planning failed. Since this is a best-effort enrichment, at minimum surface the failure as a warning (or in `data`) instead of dropping it.
- [x] **M129** (medium/performance) `internal/tools/security.go:121` - The `path` branch bounds memory with `io.LimitReader(f, securityScanLimit+1)`, but the artifact branch calls `ReadArtifact`, which uses `os.ReadFile` (or an unbounded remote `objects.Get`) and materializes the entire artifact before the caller truncates it. A large stored artifact therefore allocates its full size (plus a `string` copy) even though only `securityScanLimit` bytes are ever scanned. Please bound the artifact read the same way as the file path (e.g. expose a size-limited read on the store, or at minimum cap the returned bytes here) so `security.review` cannot be turned into an OOM vector by pointing it at a big artifact.
- [x] **M130** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/security.go:187` - The 0x200B–0x200F range also covers U+200C ZWNJ and U+200D ZWJ, which are legitimate in Arabic/Indic/Persian shaping and ubiquitous in emoji ZWJ sequences (e.g. 👨‍👩‍👧‍👦), and U+FEFF frequently appears as an artifact-encoding BOM. Such benign input will be reported as a `medium` invisible-unicode finding, which undermines the signal of this detector. Consider excluding U+200C/U+200D (or downgrading them to a `low`/informational severity) so only direction/override/tag controls raise a finding.
- [x] **M131** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/system.go:170` - `source` is taken verbatim from the caller without validating it against the documented allowlist (accepted | codex-correction | normal-teacher | fallback). An unrecognised value is persisted into fly-memory.json and the JSONL logs, and `applyDopamine` silently hits its `default` branch (no reward/punishment applied, `PAM`/`PPL1` untouched) — yet the handler still returns a hardcoded `"learned": true`, so callers believe the router learned when it did not. Validate `source` and only report `learned: true` when the router actually applied the feedback (e.g. surface the `applyDopamine` result / storage size).
- [x] **M132** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/telegram.go:167` - Non-map entries in `commands` are silently skipped with `continue`, so a malformed list such as `[{...}, "oops"]` is accepted and the tool reports success with fewer commands than the caller supplied. This masks invalid input (the caller believes all commands were registered). Return an error for non-object entries instead of dropping them.
- [x] **M133** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/util.go:123` - The allow-list stops at 0xFFFD, so every character in U+10000–U+10FFFF (emoji, rare CJK, math alphanumerics — all legal XML 1.0 chars) falls into the empty `default` branch and is silently deleted. Callers feed this directly with user-controlled text (e.g. `renderChartSVG` titles from tool args, report headings/paragraphs in extras.go), so a title like "Трек 🚀" loses the emoji without any error. Add the supplementary-plane range to the allow-list.
- [x] **M134** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/xlsx.go:212` - A `<sheet>` whose relationship id cannot be resolved is dropped here without any record, and `normalizeTarget` does not resolve relative or `../` targets (a valid OPC target such as `../xl/worksheets/sheet1.xml` becomes `xl/../xl/worksheets/sheet1.xml` and never matches a zip entry). Since the `sheetFiles` fallback in `readXLSXSheets`/`readXLSXTable` only runs when `refs` is completely empty, a workbook that declares N sheets can silently be read as N-1 sheets with no warning. Return the unresolved names (or a warning) so callers can detect the missing sheets, and normalise `..`/`.` segments in `normalizeTarget`.
- [x] **M135** (medium/bug) `internal/tools/xlsx.go:290` - `io.LimitReader` turns a size-limit overrun into a plain `io.EOF`, which the decode loop above treats as a normal end of document (the same pattern is used for `maxXLSXSharedBytes` in `readSharedStrings`). When the part is cut exactly at a token boundary the truncated worksheet/sharedStrings is accepted as complete and no warning or error is produced, so callers silently get partial data (rows/cells beyond the cut are dropped). Detect limit exhaustion explicitly, e.g. wrap the reader so that a read returning 0 bytes at the limit yields a distinct `errTooLarge` that the loop converts into `fmt.Errorf("worksheet exceeds %d bytes", maxXLSXSheetBytes)`.
- [x] **M136** (medium/bug) `plugins/hackathonkit/scripts/hackathonkit-mcp-launcher.sh:6` - The docker-compose.yml prerequisite gates *both* branches, including the local bin/hackathonkit-mcp binary that does not need Docker or the compose file at all. This contradicts the stated intent (prefer the local binary, otherwise fall back to docker compose) and causes the standalone binary to refuse to run where only the binary was installed. Move this check into the docker-compose fallback path so it only runs when the binary is absent.
- [x] **M137** (medium/bug) `plugins/hackathonkit/scripts/hooks.js:58` - `process.cwd()` throws (`ENOENT: uv_cwd`) when the current working directory has been removed, and `repoRoot()` is invoked unguarded at module load (`const ROOT = repoRoot();`). Any such throw produces a non-zero exit, breaking the file's explicit contract that "every failure is swallowed and the process exits 0". Wrap the root resolution (and the top-level `ROOT` computation) in try/catch and degrade to the plugin-relative path.
- [x] **M138** (medium/bug) `plugins/hackathonkit/scripts/hooks.js:65` - Falling back to `process.cwd()` means `CONTROL_DIR`/`SESSIONS_DIR` and `logs/*.jsonl` are created relative to whatever directory the hook happens to run in (`$HOME`, `/`, a user project, ...) even though no repo was found. This silently pollutes arbitrary directories and makes `readActive()`/`lastActiveID()` operate on unrelated state. Prefer returning a sentinel (e.g. `""`) and making `writeActive`/`appendJSONL` no-op (or log to stderr once) when no validated root exists, instead of writing into an unvalidated CWD.
- [x] **M139** (medium/bug) `plugins/hackathonkit/scripts/hooks.js:87` - Slicing stdin at 8192 bytes truncates larger hook payloads, so `JSON.parse` fails, `payloadSessionID()` returns `""` and `toolName()` falls back to the loose `"tool_name"` regex — the session id is silently lost. Combined with the `lastActiveID()` fallback this attributes the tool-use event to a different concurrent session. Read the whole payload (stream it or use a much larger bound) and, if truncation is unavoidable, at least extract `session_id` from the raw prefix instead of dropping it.
- [x] **M140** (medium/bug) `plugins/hackathonkit/scripts/hooks.js:202` - `readActive()` sorts by `started_at` (latest *started*, not latest activity), so `lastActiveID()` can pick a concurrent session that is not the one emitting the current `tool-use`; with no session id in the payload the event is mis-attributed. Additionally, expiry is computed from `started_at` alone (`now - started > SESSION_TTL_MS`), so a genuinely long-running session (>12h) has its state file unlinked mid-session and then all its events lose their id. Consider ranking by `mtime` (last activity) and refreshing `started_at`/using mtime for the TTL check.
- [x] **M141** (medium/bug) `scripts/coverage-check.sh:8` - Target validation is incomplete. The glob only rejects characters outside [0-9.], so malformed values like '1.2.3' or '.' pass this check. The subsequent awk range test then coerces them (`1.2.3 + 0` -> 1.2, `. + 0` -> 0), so a nonsense threshold is silently accepted and compared as a different number. Tighten the pattern (reject multiple dots and a lone dot).
- [x] **M142** (medium/bug) `scripts/coverage-check.sh:24` - The `*-buildvcs*` pattern matches any inherited value, including `-buildvcs=true`, so an existing setting that enables VCS stamping is left untouched — defeating the comment's stated goal of always disabling it. Only skip the append when it is already disabled; otherwise append `-buildvcs=false` (the later flag wins in Go's flag parsing).
- [x] **M143** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/coverage-check.sh:32` - Failure diagnostics can be empty. Only lines matching 'FAIL|--- FAIL' are printed, so failures that manifest as a build/compile error, a panic, or output that doesn't use those tokens produce 'test suite failed' with no context — exactly when the user most needs it. Print the captured output (or at least the tail of it) instead of, or in addition to, the grep filter.
- [x] **M144** (medium/security) `scripts/docker-check.sh:38` - `cp` copies `.env.example`'s permission bits onto the new `.env`, and per the comment below the script relies on `env-set.sh` to *preserve* the existing mode. A git-tracked `.env.example` is typically 0644, so the freshly created secrets file (later filled with DB/MinIO credentials) stays world-readable — and `env-set.sh` faithfully preserves that mode instead of tightening it. Also, `.env.example` existence is never verified: if it is missing, `cp` fails under `set -e` and the run aborts with a confusing error/exit 1. Create the file with private permissions and a clear check.
- [x] **M145** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/docker-check.sh:80` - Nothing is ever torn down: `docker compose up -d` starts postgres/redis/minio (plus anonymous volumes) and there is no `docker compose down`, so repeated runs inherit stale containers/state and leak running resources. A previous run's leftovers can mask a broken service definition (the container already exists and stays healthy) or hold ports/volumes. Consider bringing the stack down on failure and/or exposing a documented reset/cleanup path (e.g. `docker compose down --remove-orphans` behind a `--clean` flag) so CI runs are isolated.
- [x] **M146** (medium/bug) `scripts/docker-check.sh:94` - The readiness gating is incomplete and environment-dependent: redis is started but never probed, the minio probe hardcodes `127.0.0.1:9000` (breaks when `MINIO_*_PORT`/`ports` are overridden in compose, or when Docker runs on a remote/rootless host), and the whole minio check is silently skipped when `curl` is absent. A partially ready stack can therefore pass the infra stage, and later stages fail with misleading errors. Probe redis as well (e.g. `docker compose exec -T redis redis-cli ping`) and make the minio probe non-optional or fail loudly when it cannot be performed.
- [x] **M147** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/doctor.sh:24` - `PLUGIN_STATE` starts as `missing`, and both parse branches silently fall back to that value: if `codex plugin list --json` prints non-JSON, a warning banner, or an unexpected schema, jq/awk find no entry and doctor reports `hackathonkit plugin ... run scripts/setup.sh` — hiding the real cause (unparseable output / schema drift). Distinguish `unparsed` from `missing` and surface the raw output tail, as `scripts/plugin-check.sh` does with its `cannot parse codex plugin list output` message.
- [x] **M148** (medium/bug) `scripts/doctor.sh:30` - Enabled-state detection requires an explicit `.enabled == true`. If the Codex schema omits the `enabled` key for installed plugins (or nests it, e.g. `config.enabled`), an installed and working plugin is reported as `installed but disabled`, `PLUGIN_OK` stays 0 and the plugin-runtime check is skipped — a false failure that sends users on a wrong troubleshooting path. Treat a missing flag as enabled (`!= false`) and/or derive install/enable state from `scripts/plugin-check.sh`, which already parses the same JSON with python3.
- [x] **M149** (medium/bug) `scripts/doctor.sh:81` - `grep -q -- '-hackathonkit-mcp$'` matches any repository whose name merely ends with that suffix (e.g. `myorg-hackathonkit-mcp`, or a stale/foreign image), so the MCP runtime check can pass on an image that is not the one built by this repo. Match the exact repository name (and optional registry/namespace prefix), or inspect the concrete image the project builds.
- [x] **M150** (medium/bug) `scripts/env-set.sh:41` - Only the first line of stdin is captured, so a multi-line value (e.g. a PEM key or a value with an embedded newline) is silently truncated to its first line and the rest is discarded. Conversely, when the value is passed as `$3` it is interpolated verbatim by `print k "=" v`, so a value containing `\n` (e.g. `FOO='a\nEVIL=1'`) injects an extra, attacker-controlled line into the env file. Either read the whole of stdin and reject embedded newlines, or validate `$3` explicitly instead of relying on `read`'s single-line semantics.
- [x] **M151** (medium/bug) `scripts/env-set.sh:47` - `readlink -f` is a GNU extension; on BSD/macOS it fails, `TARGET` stays empty and `FILE` keeps the symlink path. Later `mv "$TMP" "$FILE"` then *replaces the symlink with a regular file*, silently breaking the link and leaving the real target stale (the earlier symlink/pid race also means the resolution, `stat`, and `mv` can act on different objects — TOCTOU). Detect this case (e.g. portable target resolution or an explicit check on the shell) and either fail with a clear message or consistently write through the link instead of swapping it out.
- [x] **M152** (medium/bug) `scripts/env-set.sh:55` - Unlike the MODE lookup, the owner lookup has no BSD/macOS fallback (`stat -f '%u:%g'`), so on those platforms `OWNER` is always empty and the later `chown` is silently skipped. Combined with `chmod/chown ... 2>/dev/null || true`, any failure to preserve ownership/mode is invisible. Mirror the MODE fallback chain and report (or at least warn about) failures.
- [x] **M153** (medium/security) `scripts/env-set.sh:58` - The temp path is fully predictable (`<file>.tmp.<pid>`) and `awk ... > "$TMP"` follows a pre-existing symlink at that path. A local attacker with write access to the directory can pre-create `$TMP` as a symlink (PIDs are guessable) and make the script truncate/overwrite an unrelated file — dangerous when this runs with elevated privileges (setup scripts call it). Use `mktemp` so the file is created with O_EXCL and private permissions.
- [x] **M154** (medium/bug) `scripts/env-set.sh:69` - This only replaces the *first* line that starts with the literal `key=`. Consequences: (1) a file that already contains two entries for the key keeps the second one, which — being later — overrides the value just written, so the command silently does not take effect; (2) common forms such as `export KEY=`, leading-whitespace ` KEY=`, or `KEY = value` are not recognized, so another `KEY=...` line is appended, producing duplicates and an unpredictable effective value; (3) a line starting with `KEY=` inside a multi-line quoted value is rewritten destructively. Consider matching those forms (or documenting/validating the accepted format) and replacing all matching occurrences.
- [x] **M155** (medium/bug) `scripts/env-set.sh:74` - This is an unlocked read-modify-write: two concurrent invocations against the same file each read the original content and then `mv` their own temp file over it, so the second `mv` silently discards the first run's update (lost update). Also, the temp name alone (`$$`) does not make the whole sequence atomic. Serialize with a lock (e.g. `mkdir "$FILE.lock"` retry loop or `flock` where available), or document that the helper is single-writer only.
- [x] **M156** (medium/test) `scripts/env-set.test.sh:23` - `file_mode` silently degrades to the literal string `unknown` when neither `stat -c` nor `stat -f` is supported. All three permission assertions then fail with a confusing `mode=unknown` detail, which looks like a permission-preservation regression rather than a tooling limitation. Detect stat support once at startup and either skip the permission assertions with a clear message or fail with `stat unsupported`.
- [x] **M157** (medium/bug) `scripts/env-set.test.sh:28` - Unguarded helper invocations abort the whole suite under `set -e`. If `env-set.sh` exits non-zero (a real regression, or an environment where its `stat`/`awk` calls fail), this line — like the calls at the `new.env`, `plain.env`, `stdin.env`, `escape.env` and `literal.env` cases — terminates the script immediately. Every later assertion and the `[fail]`/`$FAIL` summary are skipped, so the failure surfaces only as a bare non-zero exit with no diagnostic. Guard each invocation and record an explicit assertion so the harness reports what actually broke.
- [x] **M158** (medium/test) `scripts/env-set.test.sh:61` - The append branch of the helper (`END { if (!done) print k "=" v }`) is only exercised against a freshly created empty file (`new.env`), never against an existing file that already holds other keys. Add a case that appends a missing key to a non-empty file and asserts both the new key and the pre-existing keys survive, otherwise the 'preserve unrelated entries while appending' path is untested.
- [x] **M159** (medium/bug) `scripts/gen-mcp-manifest.ps1:6` - The guard tests `$PSCommandPath` but the fallback uses `$PSScriptRoot`. In the contexts where `$PSCommandPath` is empty (interactive session, dot-sourced script) `$PSScriptRoot` is empty as well, so `Join-Path "" ".."` resolves relative to the current working directory and the script can target (or fail to find) the wrong repository instead of the one containing this script. Guard on the variable actually used.
- [x] **M160** (medium/bug) `scripts/gen-mcp-manifest.ps1:22` - `Get-Content -Raw` returns `$null` for a zero-byte template, so the `.Replace(...)` calls below fail with the cryptic 'You cannot call a method on a null-valued expression' rather than the intended diagnostic. Validate the template content before using it (e.g. `if (-not $template) { Write-Error "[mcp-manifest] template is empty: $templatePath" -ErrorAction Continue; exit 1 }`).
- [x] **M161** (medium/bug) `scripts/gen-mcp-manifest.ps1:34` - Only a leftover `{{` is detected; the rendered document is never parsed as JSON, so a malformed template (or an unexpected escaping result) silently overwrites the previous good manifest with invalid JSON, breaking every MCP client that reads it. Validate the rendered content before publishing it, and check the in-memory `$manifest` instead of re-reading the file just written (also avoids re-reading another process's temp file). Note that `{{MCP_LAUNCHER}}` is spliced raw — safe only because the value is a hard-coded literal; if it ever becomes a variable it must be JSON-escaped like `$rootJson`.
- [x] **M162** (medium/security) `scripts/gen-mcp-manifest.sh` - The temp path is fully predictable (`PID`-based) and lives inside the repository tree. If an attacker or a leftover file/symlink already exists at that path, the `> "$TMP"` redirection follows the symlink and the script overwrites an arbitrary file, then `mv`s it over the manifest. Use `mktemp` (with a fallback) so the file is created atomically and exclusively: ```sh TMP="$(mktemp "$ROOT/plugins/hackathonkit/.mcp.json.tmp.XXXXXX")" || exit 1 trap 'rm -f "$TMP"' EXIT INT TERM ```
- [x] **M163** (medium/bug) `scripts/gen-mcp-manifest.sh:17` - `json_escape` only handles backslash, double quote and a literal tab. Any other C0 control character (e.g. `\f`, `\v`, `\b`, or other bytes < 0x20) is passed through unescaped and produces invalid JSON, while CR/LF are silently *deleted* by `tr -d` instead of being escaped (`\r` / `\n`), silently corrupting the value rather than failing loudly. Since the result is embedded directly into the generated manifest, escape all control characters (or reject values containing them) so the output is always valid JSON.
- [x] **M164** (medium/bug) `scripts/gen-mcp-manifest.sh:40` - The post-render check is both too broad and too weak: it aborts a perfectly valid manifest whenever the *root path* legitimately contains `{{` (e.g. a directory literally named `{{x}}`), yet it never verifies the result is actually valid JSON, so malformed output caused by escaping gaps is still written to disk. Prefer checking for the specific unresolved placeholders and, when a JSON parser is available, validating the rendered file before `mv`, e.g.: ```sh if grep -q '{{REPO_ROOT}}\|{{MCP_LAUNCHER}}' "$TMP"; then echo "[mcp-manifest] unexpanded placeholder remains; keeping the previous manifest" >&2 exit 1 fi if command -v python3 >/dev/null 2>&1; then python3 -c 'import json,sys; json.load(open(sys.argv[1]))' "$TMP" || { echo "[mcp-manifest] generated manifest is not valid JSON" >&2; exit 1; } fi ```
- [x] **M165** (medium/bug) `scripts/plugin-check.ps1:3` - Invoking codex through `cmd /c "... 2>nul"` ties the check to Windows and only resolves codex if it is on cmd's PATH; when codex is a PowerShell shim/alias or the script runs under pwsh on macOS/Linux, the output is empty and the check fails with a misleading message. Suppressed stderr hides the real cause, and exit 2 (skipped) is not distinguished. Invoke the command directly, capture stderr, and honor the documented exit-code contract. Affects scripts/plugin-check.ps1 and scripts/doctor.ps1.
- [x] **M166** (medium/bug) `scripts/plugin-check.ps1:19` - Externally produced JSON (plugin .mcp.json, install-state, codex plugin list output) is consumed without shape validation: a null/empty source path or command throws a raw parameter-binding/invocation error outside try/catch, and iterating a missing/renamed property silently skips the check and reports a misleading default verdict. Validate the parsed structure and required fields before use. Affects scripts/plugin-check.ps1 and scripts/doctor.ps1.
- [x] **M167** (medium/bug) `scripts/plugin-check.ps1:38` - The temp directory is created before the try block and only removed on the happy path after the child process finishes. If `& $cmd` (or a later statement) throws, the exception skips `Remove-Item` and the directory leaks under `$env:TEMP`; `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue` additionally masks removal failures. Move creation inside a try/finally whose finally always removes the directory, matching the sh version's `TemporaryDirectory`.
- [x] **M168** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/plugin-check.sh:10` - `|| true` swallows the `codex` exit status and `2>/dev/null` discards its diagnostics. A genuine `codex` failure (auth error, corrupt plugin db, CLI crash) therefore produces empty output and is reported by the next block as the generic `"codex plugin list is unavailable"` (exit 1), and the distinction between "not installed" (skip-ish) and "command failed" (real failure) is lost. Capture stderr/exit status and report them, e.g. `JSON="$(codex plugin list --json 2>/tmp/...)" || { ... }` so the real cause is surfaced instead of a misleading message.
- [x] **M169** (medium/bug) `scripts/plugin-check.sh:36` - `data` is assumed to be a JSON object. If `codex plugin list --json` emits a top-level array (or `"installed"` is `null`), `data.get(...)` raises `AttributeError`/`TypeError`, producing an uncaught traceback instead of the documented `[plugin-check]` failure message. Validate the shape and default safely.
- [x] **M170** (medium/bug) `scripts/plugin-check.sh:48` - A relative `source.path` from the manifest is resolved against the shell's invocation directory, not the installed plugin location. If `codex` ever reports a relative path (or a path containing `~`), `os.path.join` yields the wrong `.mcp.json` and the check falsely reports it missing. Normalize the path before joining.
- [x] **M171** (medium/security) `scripts/plugin-check.sh:73` - The command and arguments are taken verbatim from the installed plugin's `.mcp.json` and executed with no trust/allow-list check, so a tampered or unexpected manifest results in arbitrary process execution (and is even the explicit purpose of this check, which may be run in CI/verification contexts). At minimum verify the manifest belongs to the expected `hackathonkit` source and consider requiring an absolute/interpreter-resolved command before executing. Note also that `server.get("env")` is ignored, so servers configured to rely on manifest-provided environment variables will falsely fail.
- [x] **M172** (medium/bug) `scripts/plugin-check.sh:76` - `subprocess.run(..., text=True)` decodes server output with strict error handling, so non-UTF-8 bytes on stdout/stderr raise `UnicodeDecodeError` (a `ValueError`, not `OSError`/`TimeoutExpired`) which escapes the surrounding `except` clauses and yields a traceback instead of the intended exit 1. Also, `proc.returncode` is never inspected, so a server that exits non-zero after emitting three plausible lines is reported as success. Use `errors="replace"` and validate the return code.
- [x] **M173** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/plugin-check.sh:86` - The check requires exactly three stdout lines and pairs them positionally with ids 1/2/3. Any server log line, JSON-RPC notification (`notifications/*`), or prompt/notice emitted on stdout makes `len(lines) != 3` and reports a false failure, even though the actual responses are correct. Match responses by `id` instead and ignore JSON objects without a matching id (or notifications lacking `id`).
- [x] **M174** (medium/bug) `scripts/quality-gate.sh:6` - A prebuilt `bin/hackathonkit-mcp` is exec'd unconditionally with no freshness check, so after source changes the gate may silently validate a stale binary (buildvcs is disabled for the temp build, so there is no embedded VCS info to detect this either). Consider rebuilding when the binary is older than the newest tracked source file, or add an explicit `--force-build`/env override.
- [x] **M175** (medium/bug) `scripts/quality-gate.sh:12` - The INT/TERM handlers only delete the temp file and then return; POSIX shells resume the script after the handler instead of re-raising the signal, so an interrupted run continues with a misleading status (and may re-use the now-deleted binary path). Keep cleanup on EXIT and make the signal traps terminate with the conventional 128+signo status so the EXIT trap performs the deletion.
- [x] **M176** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/repair.ps1:19` - Failures are swallowed and reported as success: with $ErrorActionPreference = "Continue", non-terminating errors from New-Item, Set-Content, Copy-Item, and Remove-Item -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue only write to the error stream (or are dropped) while the script continues and prints "done"/"created". Directory creation, JSON writes (also BOM under Windows PowerShell 5.1), .env copying, and skills deletion can silently fail or be partially applied, and the exit status of write-install-state.ps1 is never checked. Use -ErrorAction Stop or explicit $?/try-catch and fail loudly. Affects scripts/repair.ps1, scripts/write-install-state.ps1, scripts/smoke.ps1, and scripts/setup.ps1.
- [x] **M177** (medium/bug) `scripts/repair.ps1:24` - JSON files are parsed with a loose Select-String regex that matches the first "profile"/"version" occurrence anywhere in the document, so a nested or duplicate key can be reported instead of the top-level value, and malformed JSON silently yields a fallback. Parse with ConvertFrom-Json and read/validate the top-level property. Affects scripts/repair.ps1, scripts/write-install-state.ps1, and scripts/doctor.ps1.
- [x] **M178** (medium/bug) `scripts/repair.ps1:35` - The rebuild block is gated only on `bin\hackathonkit-mcp.exe` being missing, yet it builds three binaries and then reports "rebuilt local binaries in bin/". If the MCP binary exists but `hackathonkit-demo-api.exe`/`hackathonkit-ctl.exe` are missing or stale, they are never rebuilt and the success message is misleading. Gate on the runtime only, or verify each expected binary individually.
- [x] **M179** (medium/bug) `scripts/repair.sh:14` - Brittle profile extraction. The leading `.*` is greedy, so if the JSON ever contains more than one `"profile": "..."` occurrence on the same line the *last* match wins, and the pattern silently fails (falling back to "full") whenever the JSON is reformatted (e.g. key and value split across lines, or no space after the colon). No diagnostic is emitted when parsing fails, so a wrong/absent profile is written back to install-state without anyone noticing. Consider parsing with a real JSON tool and warning when the key exists but cannot be read.
- [x] **M180** (medium/bug) `scripts/repair.sh:20` - Runtime detection is incomplete/unbounded: `docker info` has no timeout and can block for a long time when the daemon is unreachable, and selecting `docker` does not verify that the required images/containers actually exist, so downstream steps may assume a usable runtime that is not there. Also note RUNTIME remains "binary" when neither Go nor Docker is available (see the warning path below).
- [x] **M181** (medium/bug) `scripts/repair.sh:29` - The three `go build` calls write directly to their final paths, so a failure part-way through (under `set -e`) leaves a partially rebuilt `bin/` with a mix of fresh and stale binaries and no cleanup/rollback. Build into a temporary directory first and move the artifacts into place only after all builds succeed (or clean `bin/` on failure) so the directory is never left inconsistent.
- [x] **M182** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/repair.sh:42` - The marketplace add failure is fully swallowed by `|| true`, and the plugin-add failure path only prints a warning without surfacing the underlying error or a non-zero exit. As a result the caller cannot distinguish "plugin reinstalled" from "silently not installed". Capture and report the real error, and return a non-zero status when the reinstall was requested but failed.
- [x] **M183** (medium/bug) `scripts/setup-resources.sh:50` - Non-interactive/piped stdin is silently degraded: `ask_yes` treats EOF as "no" and the script then always sends six explicit `false` booleans, so any pre-existing profile (e.g. in CI or a scripted run where stdin is a pipe) is unconditionally overwritten with an all-false profile. Consider refusing to run (or requiring an explicit flag) when stdin is not a tty.
- [x] **M184** (medium/bug) `scripts/setup-resources.sh:63` - The shell-side track filter is much looser than the backend contract. `resources.LookupTrack` (internal/resources/resources.go) only matches a two-digit id like `08`/`08-innovation` or a known slug/label, but these patterns also accept `0`, `8`, `00`, `99`, `99-anything`, etc. Such a value passes the `case` filter, then `mcp --resources-set` fails with "unknown track", and because of `set -e` the whole questionnaire aborts at the end instead of taking the intended "ignoring invalid track" path — losing every answer the user just gave. Tighten the numeric pattern to 01-10.
- [x] **M185** (medium/bug) `scripts/setup-resources.sh:79` - The profile is persisted before any secret is stored. Under `set -e` an abort (Ctrl-C, `env-set.sh` failure, unwritable .env) after this call leaves `configs/resources.json` claiming providers whose keys were never written, with no rollback/resumability. Prefer collecting the secret values first, writing them with `set_key`, and calling `mcp --resources-set` last so the profile is only recorded once the corresponding keys are in .env.
- [x] **M186** (medium/bug) `scripts/setup.ps1:49` - `docker compose up -d` returns as soon as the containers are created, not when they are healthy. Nothing waits for postgres/redis/minio readiness, so the immediately following steps (`write-install-state`, plugin registration, and any subsequent user workflow) can run against services that are still starting. Add a readiness gate (`--wait` on Compose v2.1.1+, or a health-check polling loop).
- [x] **M187** (medium/bug) `scripts/setup.ps1:57` - The binary fallback hardcodes the Windows `.exe` suffix and no platform/architecture check is performed. On non-Windows `pwsh` (Linux/macOS, where this script is also runnable) the produced binaries are named `*.exe` and will not be found/executed by later steps. Derive the suffix from the host platform.
- [x] **M188** (medium/security) `scripts/setup.sh:42` - The permission hardening only runs when `.env` is freshly created. A pre-existing `.env` that the user (or an earlier unhardened run/editor) left at 0644 keeps its world-readable mode, exposing `OPENAI_API_KEY`, `TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN`, `POSTGRES_PASSWORD`, etc. to every local user. Move the `chmod 600` outside the `if` so the mode is enforced on every run, and consider warning when the mode has to be relaxed.
- [x] **M189** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/setup.sh:91` - Both `codex plugin remove` and `codex plugin marketplace add` discard stdout/stderr and force exit status 0. If the marketplace registration genuinely fails (e.g. plugin manifest missing, CLI too old, wrong root), the only signal the user gets is the generic "could not install the plugin automatically" message, with no diagnostic. Capture the output and surface it on failure instead of blanket `|| true`, e.g. store it in a variable and print it when the subsequent `codex plugin add` fails.
- [x] **M190** (medium/bug) `scripts/smoke.ps1:26` - `Kill()` is asynchronous and is not followed by `WaitForExit()`, so the terminating process may still hold the redirected output handle when the `finally` block calls `Remove-Item`. That removal can fail and `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue` hides the leaked temp files. Cancel synchronously and give the OS a moment to release the handles. Also note the two temp files are created *before* the `try`, so an exception from the second `GetTempFileName()`/`Set-Content` can leak the first file; create both inside a guarded region.
- [x] **M191** (medium/bug) `scripts/smoke.ps1:69` - These checks test the value of $obj.result instead of whether the property exists, so a legitimately falsy JSON-RPC result (0, false, "", empty array) is treated as a missing payload and produces a false failure. The shell counterpart tests for key presence. Test property presence and match responses by id (skipping notifications) rather than position. Affects scripts/smoke.ps1 and scripts/plugin-check.ps1.
- [x] **M192** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/smoke.sh:25` - Both `run_binary` and `run_compose` discard server stderr, and because the script runs under `set -e`, a non-zero exit from the pipeline aborts the script **silently**. A missing docker daemon, a crash on startup, a bad `--repo-root`, or a protocol error all surface as an opaque "handshake FAILED" (or nothing at all) with no root cause. Capture stderr to a temp file and dump it on failure instead of throwing it away.
- [x] **M193** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/smoke.sh:48` - In `auto` mode any executable `bin/hackathonkit-mcp` wins over the container with no build-freshness check, so a stale local binary can mask regressions that only reproduce in the compose runtime (and the chosen transport is never logged). Consider printing which transport was selected and/or allowing an explicit override / staleness check.
- [x] **M194** (medium/bug) `scripts/smoke.sh:60` - Counting raw text lines is brittle: grep -c assumes one complete JSON object per line and that the literal `"jsonrpc"` never appears inside a result payload, so a pretty-printed or payload-containing response yields a wrong count. The `|| true` also masks a real grep/IO failure as "0 lines". The hard-coded `3` duplicates the request count and the `expected = [1, 2, 3]` list, so they can drift apart. Prefer validating the actual parsed responses instead of counting text.
- [x] **M195** (medium/test) `scripts/smoke.test.sh:75` - The `--auto` case only asserts the local binary was used. It does not assert docker was skipped, does not check the exit status, and never exercises the fallback branch when `bin/hackathonkit-mcp` is missing (where `--auto` must fall through to docker). Add the mirror assertion (`grep -q '^docker$'` must be absent), capture the exit code, and add a case with `$REPO/bin/hackathonkit-mcp` removed to prove docker fallback works.
- [x] **M196** (medium/test) `scripts/smoke.test.sh:79` - This failure-mode test also only checks for a non-zero status. A command-not-found (127) or any other failure would count as a pass, so it does not actually verify the intended "docker CLI missing -> loud failure" branch. Capture the exit code (expect 1, per smoke.sh) and assert the stderr diagnostic, and make the check independent of `command -v` name-resolution semantics.
- [x] **M197** (medium/test) `scripts/smoke.test.sh:86` - The comment/description says `expected exit 2`, but the assertion only distinguishes zero from non-zero, so a regression returning 1/127 instead of the documented exit code 2 would still pass. Assert the exact status code.
- [x] **M198** (medium/bug) `scripts/sync-harness.ps1:26` - No guard on the copy source and no post-copy verification: if `plugins\hackathonkit\skills` is missing or empty the script either fails with an opaque `Get-ChildItem` error or silently swaps in an empty mirror (the required-file list does not cover the skills tree). sync-skills.ps1 explicitly checks its source and compares source/copied directory counts before swapping; mirror that here so a partial mirror can never be published.
- [x] **M199** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/sync-harness.ps1:47` - Parity gap with scripts/sync-harness.sh: the shell version additionally validates that the JSON manifests (`.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json`, `.opencode/opencode.json`, `plugins/hackathonkit/hooks/codex-hooks.json`) parse, whereas here only existence is checked — a truncated/corrupt manifest passes and is reported as "adapters ready". Validate them with `ConvertFrom-Json` inside `try/catch` and count failures into `$Missing`.
- [x] **M200** (medium/bug) `scripts/sync-harness.sh:4` - `dirname -- "$0"` resolves to `.` when the script is invoked via PATH (e.g. `sync-harness.sh` found on $PATH), since `$0` is then just the bare command name. ROOT becomes the parent of the caller's cwd, so `sync-skills.sh` and all manifest checks run against the wrong tree. Resolve the script location via `command -v` when `$0` has no directory component.
- [x] **M201** (medium/bug) `scripts/sync-harness.sh:12` - The `INT`/`TERM` trap only removes the temp dir; it does not terminate the script, so on SIGINT/SIGTERM execution may continue with a half-applied swap. Additionally the `.tmp.$$` name relies on the PID and has no mkdir-based uniqueness guard, so concurrent runs (or a stale leftover) can collide. Use distinct EXIT vs. signal handlers that re-raise the signal after cleanup, and create the tmp dir with a uniqueness check.
- [x] **M202** (medium/bug) `scripts/sync-harness.sh:39` - JSON manifest validation silently no-ops when python3 is absent, so in an environment without python3 malformed committed manifests pass this script undetected — the exact failure the check is meant to catch. Make a JSON validator mandatory (fail if none is available) or document/assert the requirement, rather than skipping validation.
- [x] **M203** (medium/bug) `scripts/sync-skills.sh:16` - The PID-suffixed temp dir only prevents temp-name collisions, not races on the shared `$DST`. Two concurrent runs can interleave their `rm -rf "$DST"` / `mv` steps: run A removes the mirror, run B swaps its copy in, then A's `mv` fails or A removes B's freshly published output, leaving a missing or inconsistent mirror. Serialize access to `$DST` with a lock (e.g. `mkdir`-based lock or `flock` where available).
- [x] **M204** (medium/bug) `scripts/sync-skills.sh:17` - For `INT`/`TERM` the trap only cleans up `$TMP` and then returns, so the shell resumes execution mid-swap (e.g. immediately after `rm -rf "$DST"`), even though the temp copy it needs was just deleted. Combined with the non-atomic swap this can leave the destination missing entirely with no rollback. The handler should also terminate the script (`exit 1`) so it never continues past a partially executed swap.
- [x] **M205** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/uninstall.ps1:9` - The root is derived by walking up two parents, so the script only works when it lives exactly at `<root>/scripts/`; any relocation (or a differently named parent directory) resolves a wrong root. The only validation is the presence of `docker-compose.yml`, which does not prove this is the intended checkout. Consider locating the root by searching upward for a marker file and/or requiring an explicit `-Root` parameter.
- [x] **M206** (medium/bug) `scripts/uninstall.ps1:17` - Native command output is discarded with `*> $null`, and $LASTEXITCODE is not reset/checked, so failed codex plugin/marketplace remove and hooks.test.js runs are reported as success with no diagnostic. Capture/forward the output, reset and check the exit code, and count failures. Affects scripts/uninstall.ps1 and scripts/doctor.ps1.
- [x] **M207** (medium/bug) `scripts/uninstall.ps1:36` - Join-Path with backslash-separated relative segments only resolves on Windows; on PowerShell Core (Linux/macOS) backslashes are literal filename characters, so targets such as plugins\hackathonkit\.mcp.json silently do not match (version falls back to 'unknown', removals become no-ops) while the script still reports success. Use forward slashes or nested Join-Path calls / [System.IO.Path]::Combine, and verify the expected targets exist. Affects scripts/uninstall.ps1, scripts/write-install-state.ps1, and scripts/sync-skills.ps1.
- [x] **M208** (medium/bug) `scripts/uninstall.sh:5` - `dirname "$0"` does not resolve symlinks, so invoking the script through a symlink or a copied launcher (e.g. `/usr/local/bin/uninstall-hackathonkit` -> this file) makes ROOT point at an unrelated directory. The guard only tests for the presence of `docker-compose.yml` and `go.mod`, which any other Go/compose project satisfies, and the script would then `rm -rf` `bin`, `.claude/skills`, etc. under that directory. Resolve the script's real path and/or assert a hackathonkit-specific marker before deleting.
- [x] **M209** (medium/bug) `scripts/uninstall.sh:28` - Both `codex` invocations swallow every error with `|| true`, yet the success line is printed unconditionally. If the CLI is not authenticated, the plugin is not registered, or the command is blocked, the user is told the plugin/marketplace were removed when nothing happened. Capture the exit status and report a warning (and consider a non-zero exit) so the dry-run/apply result is trustworthy.
- [x] **M210** (medium/bug) `scripts/uninstall.sh:46` - With `set -e`, the first failing `rm -rf` aborts the script immediately, leaving a half-uninstalled tree (e.g. plugin removed but artifacts still present) with no indication of which targets were removed. `scripts/uninstall.ps1` deliberately tracks failures per target and exits 1 at the end; the shell version should do the same for parity and predictability.
- [x] **M211** (medium/bug) `scripts/validate-skills.sh:12` - The `go run` fallback replaces the shell via `exec`, so any toolchain failure (module download/network error, compile error, VCS stamping failure) surfaces as a raw, unlabelled `go` error with no `[validate-skills]` context — and CI cannot tell "validator failed" from "validator could not be built". The sibling `scripts/quality-gate.sh` deliberately builds a temp binary with `-buildvcs=false` and prints a labelled message on failure; mirroring that here keeps the two gates consistent and gives actionable diagnostics.
- [x] **M212** (medium/maintainability) `scripts/write-install-state.ps1:25` - These component flags are hardcoded, so the emitted install-state.json always claims hooks/agents/learning are installed and lists all four adapters regardless of what was actually installed. Downstream consumers using this snapshot for resource-aware planning will get a misleading picture. Derive these values from the real installation (or accept them as parameters) instead of hardcoding.
- [x] **M213** (medium/bug) `scripts/write-install-state.sh:10` - The `sed` pattern `s/.*"version": *"\([^"]*\)".*/\1/p` is greedy and matches any line containing `"version":`, keeping only the first match via `head -n 1`. If the manifest nests a `version` field (e.g. inside a dependency/plugin block) before the top-level one, or has multiple matches on a line, the wrong value is silently extracted. Anchor the match to the top-level key.
- [x] **M214** (medium/bug) `scripts/write-install-state.sh:18` - `find | wc -l` masks `find` failures: the pipeline exit status comes from `tr`, so `set -e` never triggers and an unreadable/partially-accessible `$ROOT/.agents/skills` silently yields `skills: 0`. Capture the find output separately and check its status so errors aren't reported as an empty install.
- [x] **M215** (medium/bug) `scripts/write-install-state.sh:22` - The state file is written non-atomically: `cat > ... <<EOF` truncates `configs/install-state.json` first, so an interrupted run (or a reader racing the write) can observe an empty/partial JSON document. Write to a temp file in the same directory and atomically `mv` it into place.
- [x] **L1** (low/performance) `.codex/agents/researcher.toml:3` - `model_reasoning_effort = "high"` is set without pinning a `model` and without any cost/latency bound, while the workflow deliberately runs one isolated high-effort pass per question (3-6 questions) plus synthesis and handoff. On whatever default model the host resolves, that multiplies token spend and wall-clock time per research request. Consider pinning `model` explicitly and/or documenting the budget so the per-question loop does not run unbounded.
- [x] **L2** (low/security) `.codex/agents/reviewer.toml:4` - `sandbox_mode = "read-only"` restricts filesystem writes, but it does not itself forbid process execution — the "do not run tests or builds" guarantee rests solely on the model obeying the prompt. If strict enforcement is required, rely on sandbox/approval settings rather than prose, and make sure the "unverified - read-only" escape hatch stays valid only while the sandbox is actually read-only.
- [x] **L3** (low/documentation) `.codex/config.toml:12` - The remediation hint uses `"off"`, but the Codex schema expresses web-search modes as `"disabled"` / `"cached"` / `"live"` (the agent files in `.codex/agents/` reference `https://developers.openai.com/codex/config-schema.json`). A reader who follows this comment and writes `web_search = "off"` may end up with an unrecognized value while believing search is disabled. Confirm the accepted token and document the correct one (or `# remove this line`).
- [x] **L4** (low/bug) `.dockerignore:4` - The `.env*` glob is intended to keep real secrets out of the context, but it also matches committed templates such as `.env.example` (the repo's setup scripts and `scripts/docker-check.sh` bootstrap `.env` from it, and the devops skill instructs keeping it in sync). If any image-based workflow or documentation step needs the template in the context, the build will fail. If only the real env files should be dropped, re-include the template explicitly.
- [x] **L5** (low/maintainability) `.dockerignore:10` - `!README.md` re-includes only the README at the context root; every nested README (e.g. under `cmd/`, `plugins/`) stays excluded by `**/*.md`. If only the root README is intended to be shipped this is fine, otherwise use `!**/README.md`. (Note the exception does work here because no parent directory of `README.md` is excluded.)
